But I don’t know if I can say it only gets heavy when I’m directly thinking about that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be at work or doing something normal, and I don’t even realize I’m thinking about her until I notice I’ve gotten quiet or I’m just kind of carrying that feeling around. Then if I stop and trace it back, it usually does seem like it circles back to her leaving, or the house feeling different, or me realizing things aren’t the same.\n\nSo… mostly, maybe. But it’s not like a switch where I think about her and then it starts, and if I don’t think about her I’m fine. It’s more like it’s in the background, and thinking about her makes it louder.\n\nDoctor: When you have a moment that feels okay, does the heaviness fully lift?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it fully lifts. That’s the part I have trouble figuring out.\n\nThere are moments where I’m distracted or something feels normal for a little while, like I’m doing a task or talking to somebody and I’m not sitting there thinking about everything. And in those moments I might feel okay-ish, or at least not as weighed down. But then if I pause, or if things get quiet, it’s like I notice it’s still there.\n\nSo… fully lift? I’m not sure. It eases up sometimes, but I don’t know that it disappears completely. It’s more like it gets pushed to the side for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I don’t know if I noticed it right away, because I still sort of go through the motions, you know? And I’ve got my little wood bench too, where I’d sand things down or work on little pieces, not anything fancy, just something to keep my hands busy. Lately I’ll look at it and it’s like… it belongs to somebody else. Same with watching the Falcons. I’ll put it on sometimes, but I’m not really in it. And after church, I used to stay around and talk, just visit with people for a while. Now I’m more likely to just leave.\n\nIt’s not that I hate any of it. It’s more like the feeling that used to be there is missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” is a strong word, I guess, and I don’t know if I’d say it clean like that.\n\nThere are times where I look at things and I think, “Well, I don’t know what this is supposed to look like now,” or I have trouble seeing how it’s going to feel normal again. But I don’t know if that’s the same as hopeless. It’s more like… I get worn down by it, and I can’t picture the next part very well.\n\nSome days I’ll think, “Maybe this is just how it is now,” and that’s not a good feeling. But then other times I know things probably do change, because they always have before. So I guess I’d say I’ve had moments that feel kind of bleak, but I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot about many different things, not just your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I worry some, like anybody does, and once I get something in my head I can chew on it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know that it’s spread out over a whole bunch of things.\n\nThe thing with my daughter leaving is the one that sticks out the most, and then other thoughts kind of branch off from that, if that makes sense. Like it’s not me sitting there worrying equally about every part of life all day long. But there were times before she left where, for more than just a few days, I felt down or kind of emptied out, and the things I usually liked didn’t have much pull.\n\nThis stretch after my daughter left, though… this has hit me the hardest. Before that, there were low periods, but I don’t think I’d call it two full years of being down most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, did you ever have a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months between those low stretches?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. So, I guess… yeah, I’ve been down a lot of the days since then. I just don’t know if I’d call it every single day or all day long.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this heaviness, is it mostly strongest when you’re thinking about your daughter being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, that’s the thing my mind goes to a lot, so I’d say it’s tied up with her being gone, yes. But I don’t know if I can say it only gets heavy when I’m directly thinking about that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be at work or doing something normal, and I don’t even realize I’m thinking about her until I notice I’ve gotten quiet or I’m just kind of carrying that feeling around. Then if I stop and trace it back, it usually does seem like it circles back to her leaving, or the house feeling different, or me realizing things aren’t the same.\n\nSo… mostly, maybe. But it’s not like a switch where I think about her and then it starts, and if I don’t think about her I’m fine. It’s more like it’s in the background, and thinking about her makes it louder.\n\nDoctor: When you have a moment that feels okay, does the heaviness fully lift?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it fully lifts. That’s the part I have trouble figuring out.\n\nThere are moments where I’m distracted or something feels normal for a little while, like I’m doing a task or talking to somebody and I’m not sitting there thinking about everything. And in those moments I might feel okay-ish, or at least not as weighed down. But then if I pause, or if things get quiet, it’s like I notice it’s still there.\n\nSo… fully lift? I’m not sure. It eases up sometimes, but I don’t know that it disappears completely. It’s more like it gets pushed to the side for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I don’t know if I noticed it right away, because I still sort of go through the motions, you know? But the things I used to look forward to just don’t pull at me the same way.\n\nLike, I used to mess with my tomato plants — I could spend a good while out there checking them, fussing with the soil, seeing what needed tying up or trimming. And I’ve got my little wood bench too, where I’d sand things down or work on little pieces, not anything fancy, just something to keep my hands busy. Lately I’ll look at it and it’s like… it belongs to somebody else. Same with watching the Falcons. I’ll put it on sometimes, but I’m not really in it. And after church, I used to stay around and talk, just visit with people for a while. Now I’m more likely to just leave.\n\nIt’s not that I hate any of it. It’s more like the feeling that used to be there is missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” is a strong word, I guess, and I don’t know if I’d say it clean like that.\n\nThere are times where I look at things and I think, “Well, I don’t know what this is supposed to look like now,” or I have trouble seeing how it’s going to feel normal again. But I don’t know if that’s the same as hopeless. It’s more like… I get worn down by it, and I can’t picture the next part very well.\n\nSome days I’ll think, “Maybe this is just how it is now,” and that’s not a good feeling. But then other times I know things probably do change, because they always have before. So I guess I’d say I’ve had moments that feel kind of bleak, but I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot about many different things, not just your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I worry some, like anybody does, and once I get something in my head I can chew on it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know that it’s spread out over a whole bunch of things.\n\nThe thing with my daughter leaving is the one that sticks out the most, and then other thoughts kind of branch off from that, if that makes sense. Like it’s not me sitting there worrying equally about every part of life all day long. It’s more like I get pulled back to that same general place.\n\nI guess I’d need to think about what counts as “many different things.” I don’t want to say no too fast, because maybe I don’t notice it when I’m doing it. But from what I can tell, it’s not like I’m constantly worrying about everything under the sun. It’s more tied to that situation, or to how things feel around it.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, had you ever gone through a period of at least a couple of weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in things most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back on it, it’s not like this just came out of nowhere the day she left. I guess I can see now there’ve been low stretches kind of coming around for maybe about a year or so.\n\nThey weren’t always the same, and I probably didn’t call them anything at the time. I’d just think, “Well, I’m tired,” or “I’m in a bad patch,” or I’d tell myself I was just getting older and things don’t feel the same. But there were times before she left where, for more than just a few days, I felt down or kind of emptied out, and the things I usually liked didn’t have much pull.\n\nThis stretch after my daughter left, though… this has hit me the hardest. That’s the one where it feels more obvious, like I can’t really explain it away as easily. But yes, if you’re asking if there were a couple-week periods before that, I’d say there probably were. Looking back, those low times had been showing up for about a year.\n\nDoctor: For at least two years, have you felt down or depressed more days than not?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s been two years. I mean, when I look back, I can see these low stretches have been coming around for maybe about a year or so, like I said. They’d sort of show up, and I’d tell myself it was just a rough patch or I was worn out or whatever.\n\nBut two years, more days than not… that feels like too much to say. It hasn’t been that long in a steady way, at least not how I remember it. The one after my daughter left is the one that really hit me the hardest, where it became harder to ignore or explain away. Before that, there were low periods, but I don’t think I’d call it two full years of being down most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, did you ever have a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months between those low stretches?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t keep track of it that neatly, you know? Like, I can remember there were stretches where I felt low, and then there were times where I was functioning more normally, going through the day, doing what I needed to do, maybe even feeling okay enough that I wasn’t thinking about it all the time.\n\nBut whether I was really “back to my usual self” for a full two months… I don’t know. That’s harder for me to say. I don’t want to just say yes if I’m filling in blanks after the fact. Some periods probably felt better than others, but I’m not sure I ever sat there thinking, “Okay, I’m completely myself again now.”\n\nI guess what I can say is it wasn’t one solid low feeling the whole time before this current period. It came and went some. But putting a two-month number on it, I’d have to think about it more. I’m not certain.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, was there a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I’m trying not to guess just to give you a neat answer. “Completely back to my usual self” is the part I get stuck on.\n\nThere were definitely times before this current depression where I was doing better than during the low stretches. I’d be getting up, handling work, talking to people, doing the normal things. From the outside, somebody might have said I seemed fine. And I probably would’ve said I was fine too, just because that’s what I tend to say.\n\nBut if you mean really fully back to how I used to feel, for at least two solid months, where I wasn’t dragging or feeling off underneath it all… I honestly don’t know. I can’t say that clearly. It wasn’t like I had it marked on a calendar, and looking back it all kind of blends together — better spells, worse spells, and then this current one being the most obvious.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I can say there were better stretches, but I don’t know if they were a full two months of feeling completely like myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, was there any stretch when you felt entirely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: I know I’m kind of circling the same answer, but honestly, I don’t have a clear one. There were times when I felt more normal, or at least I was acting more normal — going to work, dealing with people, doing the regular routine — but “entirely back to my normal self” is hard for me to say.\n\nI don’t remember a stretch where I could confidently say, yes, that was me fully back, no question. It’s more like some days or weeks were better and I wasn’t thinking about it as much, and then later I’d realize I still didn’t feel quite right. Looking back, it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t want to tell you yes if I’m only guessing.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, did you ever return fully to your usual self between the low periods?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I’m really not trying to. I could go to work, take care of things, talk normally enough, and from the outside it probably looked like I had bounced back. But whether I felt fully like myself inside, the way I think you mean it… I’m not confident saying yes.\n\nIt wasn’t like there was a clear moment where I thought, “Okay, I’m back now.” It was more uneven than that — better for a while, then not as good, then maybe a little better again. So the honest answer is I don’t really know if I ever returned fully to my usual self between those low stretches. I can say I had better periods, but I can’t say they were fully normal.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, did you ever have an earlier period when the depression became severe enough that you had thoughts that you might be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s actually why I ended up coming in now. I don’t know how to separate “earlier” from what’s been happening recently, because the thing that scared me was I kept having this thought that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI wasn’t saying it out loud at first. I was kind of carrying it around and trying to brush it off like, “Well, I’m just tired,” or “I’m just having a bad day.” But it kept coming back, and it scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor. I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I know I kind of said this before, but that’s the hard part for me — I can remember being better, but I can’t say I felt completely back to normal.

There were times between the lows where I could function more like myself. I’d get up, go do what I had to do, talk to people, take care of things, and I wasn’t feeling crushed down every minute. But it got to the point where I couldn’t just brush it off like I usually do. It wasn’t just a bad mood or being tired. It was that thought coming back, like maybe they’d have less to deal with if I wasn’t around, and that really shook me.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now… I don’t know that I’d say I’m sitting here planning to do it. It’s more that the thought keeps coming back, like, “maybe they’d be better off if I just didn’t wake up.” And that scared me enough that I told my supervisor, because I don’t usually say things like that out loud. \n\nIt’s not something I wanted to admit. I kept thinking I could push it aside, but it kept showing up, and then I started worrying that just having that thought so much meant something was really wrong.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific stressful event happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s been about two weeks, maybe right around then, since my daughter left Georgia for the military. That’s when I really noticed this whole change in me starting.\n\nI mean, I don’t know if it’s fair to say it was only that, but that’s the thing that happened, and after that I just wasn’t myself. I kept trying to tell myself, you know, she’s doing what she’s supposed to do, she’s grown, but it hit me harder than I expected. Since she left, these past couple weeks have just felt different in a bad way.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve felt off most days, yeah, since she left. But “depressed” is a word I don’t always know how to use right, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like I get up and I do what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this heaviness sitting on me. Some days it’s worse than others. I keep thinking about her being gone and the house feeling different, and then I start wondering why I’m reacting like this when other people have kids leave home all the time. So, I guess… yeah, I’ve been down a lot of the days since then. I just don’t know if I’d call it every single day or all day long.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this heaviness, is it mostly strongest when you’re thinking about your daughter being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, that’s the thing my mind goes to a lot, so I’d say it’s tied up with her being gone, yes. But I don’t know if I can say it only gets heavy when I’m directly thinking about that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be at work or doing something normal, and I don’t even realize I’m thinking about her until I notice I’ve gotten quiet or I’m just kind of carrying that feeling around. Then if I stop and trace it back, it usually does seem like it circles back to her leaving, or the house feeling different, or me realizing things aren’t the same.\n\nSo… mostly, maybe. But it’s not like a switch where I think about her and then it starts, and if I don’t think about her I’m fine. It’s more like it’s in the background, and thinking about her makes it louder.\n\nDoctor: When you have a moment that feels okay, does the heaviness fully lift?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it fully lifts. That’s the part I have trouble figuring out.\n\nThere are moments where I’m distracted or something feels normal for a little while, like I’m doing a task or talking to somebody and I’m not sitting there thinking about everything. And in those moments I might feel okay-ish, or at least not as weighed down. But then if I pause, or if things get quiet, it’s like I notice it’s still there.\n\nSo… fully lift? I’m not sure. It eases up sometimes, but I don’t know that it disappears completely. It’s more like it gets pushed to the side for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I don’t know if I noticed it right away, because I still sort of go through the motions, you know? But the things I used to look forward to just don’t pull at me the same way.\n\nLike, I used to mess with my tomato plants — I could spend a good while out there checking them, fussing with the soil, seeing what needed tying up or trimming. And I’ve got my little wood bench too, where I’d sand things down or work on little pieces, not anything fancy, just something to keep my hands busy. Lately I’ll look at it and it’s like… it belongs to somebody else. Same with watching the Falcons. I’ll put it on sometimes, but I’m not really in it. And after church, I used to stay around and talk, just visit with people for a while. Now I’m more likely to just leave.\n\nIt’s not that I hate any of it. It’s more like the feeling that used to be there is missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” is a strong word, I guess, and I don’t know if I’d say it clean like that.\n\nThere are times where I look at things and I think, “Well, I don’t know what this is supposed to look like now,” or I have trouble seeing how it’s going to feel normal again. But I don’t know if that’s the same as hopeless. It’s more like… I get worn down by it, and I can’t picture the next part very well.\n\nSome days I’ll think, “Maybe this is just how it is now,” and that’s not a good feeling. But then other times I know things probably do change, because they always have before. So I guess I’d say I’ve had moments that feel kind of bleak, but I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot about many different things, not just your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I worry some, like anybody does, and once I get something in my head I can chew on it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know that it’s spread out over a whole bunch of things.\n\nThe thing with my daughter leaving is the one that sticks out the most, and then other thoughts kind of branch off from that, if that makes sense. Like it’s not me sitting there worrying equally about every part of life all day long. It’s more like I get pulled back to that same general place.\n\nI guess I’d need to think about what counts as “many different things.” I don’t want to say no too fast, because maybe I don’t notice it when I’m doing it. But from what I can tell, it’s not like I’m constantly worrying about everything under the sun. It’s more tied to that situation, or to how things feel around it.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, had you ever gone through a period of at least a couple of weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in things most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back on it, it’s not like this just came out of nowhere the day she left. I guess I can see now there’ve been low stretches kind of coming around for maybe about a year or so.\n\nThey weren’t always the same, and I probably didn’t call them anything at the time. I’d just think, “Well, I’m tired,” or “I’m in a bad patch,” or I’d tell myself I was just getting older and things don’t feel the same. But there were times before she left where, for more than just a few days, I felt down or kind of emptied out, and the things I usually liked didn’t have much pull.\n\nThis stretch after my daughter left, though… this has hit me the hardest. That’s the one where it feels more obvious, like I can’t really explain it away as easily. But yes, if you’re asking if there were a couple-week periods before that, I’d say there probably were. Looking back, those low times had been showing up for about a year.\n\nDoctor: For at least two years, have you felt down or depressed more days than not?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s been two years. I mean, when I look back, I can see these low stretches have been coming around for maybe about a year or so, like I said. They’d sort of show up, and I’d tell myself it was just a rough patch or I was worn out or whatever.\n\nBut two years, more days than not… that feels like too much to say. It hasn’t been that long in a steady way, at least not how I remember it. The one after my daughter left is the one that really hit me the hardest, where it became harder to ignore or explain away. Before that, there were low periods, but I don’t think I’d call it two full years of being down most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, did you ever have a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months between those low stretches?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t keep track of it that neatly, you know? Like, I can remember there were stretches where I felt low, and then there were times where I was functioning more normally, going through the day, doing what I needed to do, maybe even feeling okay enough that I wasn’t thinking about it all the time.\n\nBut whether I was really “back to my usual self” for a full two months… I don’t know. That’s harder for me to say. I don’t want to just say yes if I’m filling in blanks after the fact. Some periods probably felt better than others, but I’m not sure I ever sat there thinking, “Okay, I’m completely myself again now.”\n\nI guess what I can say is it wasn’t one solid low feeling the whole time before this current period. It came and went some. But putting a two-month number on it, I’d have to think about it more. I’m not certain.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, was there a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I’m trying not to guess just to give you a neat answer. “Completely back to my usual self” is the part I get stuck on.\n\nThere were definitely times before this current depression where I was doing better than during the low stretches. I’d be getting up, handling work, talking to people, doing the normal things. From the outside, somebody might have said I seemed fine. And I probably would’ve said I was fine too, just because that’s what I tend to say.\n\nBut if you mean really fully back to how I used to feel, for at least two solid months, where I wasn’t dragging or feeling off underneath it all… I honestly don’t know. I can’t say that clearly. It wasn’t like I had it marked on a calendar, and looking back it all kind of blends together — better spells, worse spells, and then this current one being the most obvious.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I can say there were better stretches, but I don’t know if they were a full two months of feeling completely like myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, was there any stretch when you felt entirely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: I know I’m kind of circling the same answer, but honestly, I don’t have a clear one. There were times when I felt more normal, or at least I was acting more normal — going to work, dealing with people, doing the regular routine — but “entirely back to my normal self” is hard for me to say.\n\nI don’t remember a stretch where I could confidently say, yes, that was me fully back, no question. It’s more like some days or weeks were better and I wasn’t thinking about it as much, and then later I’d realize I still didn’t feel quite right. Looking back, it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t want to tell you yes if I’m only guessing.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, did you ever return fully to your usual self between the low periods?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I’m really not trying to. It’s just hard to separate out in my memory.\n\nBetween the low periods, there were definitely times where I was more functional, where I was getting through my days and maybe not feeling as weighed down. I could go to work, take care of things, talk normally enough, and from the outside it probably looked like I had bounced back. But whether I felt fully like myself inside, the way I think you mean it… I’m not confident saying yes.\n\nIt wasn’t like there was a clear moment where I thought, “Okay, I’m back now.” It was more uneven than that — better for a while, then not as good, then maybe a little better again. So the honest answer is I don’t really know if I ever returned fully to my usual self between those low stretches. I can say I had better periods, but I can’t say they were fully normal.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, did you ever have an earlier period when the depression became severe enough that you had thoughts that you might be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s actually why I ended up coming in now. I don’t know how to separate “earlier” from what’s been happening recently, because the thing that scared me was I kept having this thought that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nI wasn’t saying it out loud at first. I was kind of carrying it around and trying to brush it off like, “Well, I’m just tired,” or “I’m just having a bad day.” But it kept coming back, and it scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor. That was not easy for me to say to anybody, especially at work, but I think part of me knew I shouldn’t just keep it to myself.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had thoughts like that — not wanting to be here, or thinking people might be better off without me. That’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, did you ever have another time when you felt this depressed for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s hard for me to put clean edges around it. Looking back, these low stretches have been coming around for maybe about a year now. It wasn’t just one bad day here and there — there were periods where I felt down for long enough that it wasn’t normal for me.\n\nBut this current one, after my daughter left, this has definitely hit me the hardest. That’s the one that feels like it really knocked the legs out from under me. The other times before, I could sort of keep moving and maybe tell myself I was just worn out or stressed, but now looking back, yeah, I think there were earlier stretches that lasted at least a couple weeks. I just didn’t label them that way at the time.\n\nDoctor: Between those earlier low periods, did you ever feel completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I know I kind of said this before, but that’s the hard part for me — I can remember being better, but I can’t say I felt completely back to normal.\n\nThere were times between the lows where I could function more like myself. I’d get up, go do what I had to do, talk to people, take care of things, and I wasn’t feeling crushed down every minute. So if someone was looking from the outside, they might have thought, “He’s fine now.” And maybe I even thought that a little bit at the time, like, “Okay, I’m doing better.”\n\nBut completely back to my usual self? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like a clean reset. It was more like I was out of the deepest part, but still not quite right, if that makes sense. Like I could keep going, but I don’t remember having that solid feeling of, “Yeah, I’m myself again.” So I guess the most honest answer is I had better stretches, yes, but I’m not sure they were completely normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I came in because I’ve been having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. It’s been about two weeks, maybe right around then, since my daughter left Georgia for the military. I kept trying to tell myself, you know, she’s doing what she’s supposed to do, she’s grown, but it hit me harder than I expected. Since she left, these past couple weeks have just felt different in a bad way.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’ve felt off most days, yeah, since she left. But “depressed” is a word I don’t always know how to use right, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like I get up and I do what I’m supposed to do, but there’s this heaviness sitting on me. Some days it’s worse than others. I keep thinking about her being gone and the house feeling different, and then I start wondering why I’m reacting like this when other people have kids leave home all the time. So, I guess… yeah, I’ve been down a lot of the days since then. I just don’t know if I’d call it every single day or all day long.\n\nDoctor: When you feel this heaviness, is it mostly strongest when you’re thinking about your daughter being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, that’s the thing my mind goes to a lot, so I’d say it’s tied up with her being gone, yes. But I don’t know if I can say it only gets heavy when I’m directly thinking about that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be at work or doing something normal, and I don’t even realize I’m thinking about her until I notice I’ve gotten quiet or I’m just kind of carrying that feeling around. Then if I stop and trace it back, it usually does seem like it circles back to her leaving, or the house feeling different, or me realizing things aren’t the same.\n\nSo… mostly, maybe. But it’s not like a switch where I think about her and then it starts, and if I don’t think about her I’m fine. It’s more like it’s in the background, and thinking about her makes it louder.\n\nDoctor: When you have a moment that feels okay, does the heaviness fully lift?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it fully lifts. That’s the part I have trouble figuring out.\n\nThere are moments where I’m distracted or something feels normal for a little while, like I’m doing a task or talking to somebody and I’m not sitting there thinking about everything. And in those moments I might feel okay-ish, or at least not as weighed down. But then if I pause, or if things get quiet, it’s like I notice it’s still there.\n\nSo… fully lift? I’m not sure. It eases up sometimes, but I don’t know that it disappears completely. It’s more like it gets pushed to the side for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I don’t know if I noticed it right away, because I still sort of go through the motions, you know? But the things I used to look forward to just don’t pull at me the same way.\n\nLike, I used to mess with my tomato plants — I could spend a good while out there checking them, fussing with the soil, seeing what needed tying up or trimming. And I’ve got my little wood bench too, where I’d sand things down or work on little pieces, not anything fancy, just something to keep my hands busy. Lately I’ll look at it and it’s like… it belongs to somebody else. Same with watching the Falcons. I’ll put it on sometimes, but I’m not really in it. And after church, I used to stay around and talk, just visit with people for a while. Now I’m more likely to just leave.\n\nIt’s not that I hate any of it. It’s more like the feeling that used to be there is missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” is a strong word, I guess, and I don’t know if I’d say it clean like that.\n\nThere are times where I look at things and I think, “Well, I don’t know what this is supposed to look like now,” or I have trouble seeing how it’s going to feel normal again. But I don’t know if that’s the same as hopeless. It’s more like… I get worn down by it, and I can’t picture the next part very well.\n\nSome days I’ll think, “Maybe this is just how it is now,” and that’s not a good feeling. But then other times I know things probably do change, because they always have before. So I guess I’d say I’ve had moments that feel kind of bleak, but I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot about many different things, not just your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I worry some, like anybody does, and once I get something in my head I can chew on it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know that it’s spread out over a whole bunch of things.\n\nThe thing with my daughter leaving is the one that sticks out the most, and then other thoughts kind of branch off from that, if that makes sense. It’s more like I get pulled back to that same general place.\n\nI guess I’d need to think about what counts as “many different things.” I don’t want to say no too fast, because maybe I don’t notice it when I’m doing it. It’s more tied to that situation, or to how things feel around it.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, had you ever gone through a period of at least a couple of weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in things most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back on it, it’s not like this just came out of nowhere the day she left. I guess I can see now there’ve been low stretches kind of coming around for maybe about a year or so.\n\nThey weren’t always the same, and I probably didn’t call them anything at the time. I’d just think, “Well, I’m tired,” or “I’m in a bad patch,” or I’d tell myself I was just getting older and things don’t feel the same. But there were times before she left where, for more than just a few days, I felt down or kind of emptied out, and the things I usually liked didn’t have much pull.\n\nThis stretch after my daughter left, though… this has hit me the hardest. That’s the one where it feels more obvious, like I can’t really explain it away as easily. But yes, if you’re asking if there were a couple-week periods before that, I’d say there probably were. Looking back, those low times had been showing up for about a year.\n\nDoctor: For at least two years, have you felt down or depressed more days than not?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s been two years. I mean, when I look back, I can see these low stretches have been coming around for maybe about a year or so, like I said. They’d sort of show up, and I’d tell myself it was just a rough patch or I was worn out or whatever.\n\nBut two years, more days than not… that feels like too much to say. It hasn’t been that long in a steady way, at least not how I remember it. The one after my daughter left is the one that really hit me the hardest, where it became harder to ignore or explain away. Before that, there were low periods, but I don’t think I’d call it two full years of being down most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, did you ever have a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months between those low stretches?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t keep track of it that neatly, you know? Like, I can remember there were stretches where I felt low, and then there were times where I was functioning more normally, going through the day, doing what I needed to do, maybe even feeling okay enough that I wasn’t thinking about it all the time.\n\nBut whether I was really “back to my usual self” for a full two months… I don’t know. That’s harder for me to say. I don’t want to just say yes if I’m filling in blanks after the fact. Some periods probably felt better than others, but I’m not sure I ever sat there thinking, “Okay, I’m completely myself again now.”\n\nI guess what I can say is it wasn’t one solid low feeling the whole time before this current period. It came and went some. But putting a two-month number on it, I’d have to think about it more. I’m not certain.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, was there a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I’m trying not to guess just to give you a neat answer. “Completely back to my usual self” is the part I get stuck on.\n\nThere were definitely times before this current depression where I was doing better than during the low stretches. I’d be getting up, handling work, talking to people, doing the normal things. From the outside, somebody might have said I seemed fine. And I probably would’ve said I was fine too, just because that’s what I tend to say.\n\nBut if you mean really fully back to how I used to feel, for at least two solid months, where I wasn’t dragging or feeling off underneath it all… I honestly don’t know. I can’t say that clearly. I can say there were better stretches, but I don’t know if they were a full two months of feeling completely like myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, was there any stretch when you felt entirely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: I know I’m kind of circling the same answer, but honestly, I don’t have a clear one. It’s more like some days or weeks were better and I wasn’t thinking about it as much, and then later I’d realize I still didn’t feel quite right. Looking back, it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t want to tell you yes if I’m only guessing.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, did you ever return fully to your usual self between the low periods?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for sure. I can say I had better periods, but I can’t say they were fully normal.\n\nDoctor: Before this current depression, did you ever have an earlier period when the depression became severe enough that you had thoughts that you might be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s actually why I ended up coming in now. That’s the one that feels like it really knocked the legs out from under me.
